<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01R4Don1H2QBZXsQLeUG61Rn`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\subsection{Theoretical Implications of $\Kempirical = 2$}
5	\label{sec:discuss_kstar}
6	
7	The Data Processing Inequality guarantees the existence of a finite $\Ktheory$ beyond which adjacent generations become indistinguishable (Section~\ref{sec:formulation}).
8	Our empirical result---$\Kempirical = 2$ uniformly across all 8 tested configurations (4 model-mode combinations from Section~\ref{sec:kstar_boundary} and 4 decoding strategies from the ablation)---establishes that this boundary is not only finite but remarkably shallow at the $\leq$1.5B parameter scale.
9	
10	The shallowness of $\Kempirical$ is explained by the rate of information loss across generations.
11	The 0v1 boundary is near-trivially separable ($\auc > 0.99$ across all configurations), reflecting the large distributional gap between human text and first-generation synthetic text.
12	At the 1v2 boundary, $\auc$ drops by approximately 30 percentage points ($\auc$ range: 0.6112--0.6955), indicating that the majority of the discriminative signal is consumed in the first generation step.
13	By the 2v3 boundary, $\auc$ falls to 0.5496--0.5893---below the operational threshold $\theta = 0.60$---and deeper pairs converge toward chance.
14	Recursive generation thus acts as a severely lossy channel whose information bottleneck is traversed within two steps.
15	
16	This pattern aligns with findings from the model collapse literature.
17	Shumailov et al.~\cite{shumailov2024collapse} observed that tail-distribution degradation is most acute in the earliest generations, with minority modes vanishing before the output distribution stabilizes.
18	Dohmatob et al.~\cite{dohmatob2024model} derived scaling laws demonstrating that tail probability mass is lost at rates predictable from model and data scale.
19	Our $\Kempirical = 2$ provides a complementary, classifier-based measurement of this phenomenon: the information-theoretic discrimination horizon is reached after just two generation steps.
20	Alemohammad et al.~\cite{alemohammad2024selfconsuming} proved convergence to a fixed point for self-consuming generative models; $\Kempirical = 2$ indicates that, operationally, the approach to this fixed point is rapid enough that adjacent-generation distinctions collapse within two steps.
21	
22	The invariance of $\Kempirical = 2$ across decoding strategies merits emphasis.
23	Greedy decoding, nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.9$), $T{=}0.7$, and $T{=}1.2$ all yield $\Kempirical = 2$, with 1v2 $\auc$ spanning a narrow range of [0.6866, 0.7039].
24	Higher decoding entropy modestly increases signal strength ($\auc_{1,2}$: 0.69 greedy $\to$ 0.70 at $T{=}1.2$) but does not shift the boundary.
25	This robustness suggests that $\Kempirical$ is governed by the fundamental information bottleneck of the generation process rather than by surface-level properties of the sampling algorithm.
26	
27	\subsection{Implications of Cross-Model Transfer}
28	\label{sec:discuss_transfer}
29	
30	The cross-model transfer results (Section~\ref{sec:transfer}) indicate that depth signals are largely encoded in text-level statistical properties rather than generator-specific artifacts.
31	Classifiers trained on Pythia-1.4B and evaluated on OLMo-1B retain 90--92\% of within-model accuracy, and the broader continuation-mode transfer matrix shows 80--92\% retention across all model pairs.
32	At the 0v1 boundary, cross-model $\auc$ exceeds 0.96 universally, confirming that binary contamination detection is fully transferable across generators.
33	
34	The practical implication is direct: a single depth classifier trained on one model's outputs can be deployed to estimate generational depth for texts produced by other models, without model-specific retraining.
35	This transferability extends the utility of depth estimation to open-web settings where the generating model is unknown---precisely the scenario where depth-aware data curation is most needed.
36	
37	Two caveats bound this conclusion.
38	First, transfer retention of 80--92\% suggests a large text-intrinsic component but does not quantify its exact share; we refrain from decomposing the signal into text-intrinsic and model-specific fractions.
39	Second, Qwen-cont$\leftrightarrow$OLMo retention (80--85\%) is lower than Pythia$\leftrightarrow$OLMo (90--92\%), indicating that model family similarity modulates transfer even among continuation-mode configurations.
40	
41	\subsection{Feature Importance Across Decoding Strategies}
42	\label{sec:discuss_features}
43	
44	While $\Kempirical = 2$ is invariant to decoding strategy (4/4 configurations), the feature importance ordering is not uniformly consistent.
45	Greedy decoding and $T{=}0.7$ share highly correlated feature rankings (Spearman $\rho = 0.914$), with top features dominated by vocabulary diversity measures: \texttt{self\_bleu}, \texttt{hapax\_ratio}, and \texttt{ttr}.
46	In contrast, greedy versus $T{=}1.2$ yields a lower correlation ($\rho = 0.53$, $p = 0.041$; suggestive but nonsignificant after Bonferroni correction for 6 pairwise comparisons), with $T{=}1.2$ promoting \texttt{hapax\_ratio}, \texttt{ttr}, and \texttt{surp\_skew} to the top ranks.
47	Notably, \texttt{freq\_entropy} rises from rank~10 under greedy ($\auc = 0.5139$) to rank~5 under $T{=}1.2$ ($\auc = 0.5905$), illustrating how high-entropy decoding amplifies frequency-distribution artifacts.
48	
49	This dissociation reveals an important distinction: the \emph{existence} of the depth boundary is robust, but the \emph{optimal feature subset} for detecting it depends on the sampling strategy.
50	High-entropy decoding amplifies frequency-distribution artifacts while attenuating vocabulary-diversity signals, shifting which text properties carry the most discriminative power without altering whether adjacent generations can be distinguished.
51	For practitioners, this implies that a universal depth classifier should incorporate features from multiple groups---surprisal moments, vocabulary diversity, and frequency tail statistics---to maintain performance across unknown decoding conditions.
52	
53	The cross-model feature ordering (Section~\ref{sec:feature_degradation}) provides a complementary perspective: among continuation-mode models, single-feature $\auc$ rankings are highly consistent (Spearman $\rho \geq 0.85$), with surprisal-variance features---\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}, \texttt{surp\_std}, \texttt{surp\_d2\_std}---dominating uniformly.
54	A genuine limitation, however, is that depth-pair-level agreement weakens to $\tau \approx 0.37$ at the 1v2 boundary (Section~\ref{sec:feature_degradation}), indicating that the relative contribution of individual features at the discrimination boundary varies across models even when the aggregate ordering is shared.
55	
56	\subsection{LLM Zero-Shot Baseline}
57	\label{sec:discuss_llm}
58	
59	Frontier LLMs provide a natural baseline for depth estimation.
60	On a 500-sample test set with 5 depth classes (depths 0--4), GPT-5.5 achieves 33.2\% accuracy (MAE = 1.10) and Claude Opus~4.6 achieves 33.1\% accuracy (MAE = 1.15)---both below the OBD-LightGBM baseline of 38.6\% (MAE = 1.11).
61	Simultaneously, both models achieve approximately 88\% binary accuracy on the human-vs.-synthetic distinction (GPT-5.5: 87.8\%, Claude: 88.2\%).
62	
63	This gap between binary detection and depth estimation is informative.
64	Frontier LLMs can reliably determine \emph{whether} a text is machine-generated but cannot estimate \emph{how many generations of recursive processing} it has undergone.
65	Depth estimation requires sensitivity to subtle distributional shifts between adjacent generations---shifts orders of magnitude smaller than the human-vs.-machine gap.
66	The 15-dimensional statistical feature pipeline, despite its simplicity, captures these fine-grained shifts more effectively than frontier LLMs in a zero-shot setting.
67	This motivates the dedicated feature-engineering approach: the task demands specialized statistical sensitivity rather than general language understanding.
68	
69	\subsection{Limitations}
70	\label{sec:limitations}
71	
72	We identify six limitations that bound the generalizability of our findings.
73	
74	\emph{Model scale.}
75	All experiments use models at or below 1.5B parameters (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct).
76	Larger models may produce higher-fidelity text with weaker depth signatures, potentially shifting $\Kempirical$ beyond~2.
77	
78	\emph{Prompt-chain versus retrain-chain.}
79	Our recursive generation protocol is a prompt-chain: the same fixed model repeatedly generates from the previous output.
80	Real-world model collapse involves retrain-chains, where models are retrained on data from earlier generations~\cite{shumailov2024collapse}.
81	Retrain-chains introduce additional degradation through training dynamics, potentially producing different $\Kstar$ values.
82	
83	\emph{Language and domain.}
84	All experiments use English text from the C4 corpus.
85	Depth dynamics may differ across languages, domains (e.g., code, scientific text), and source corpora with different distributional properties.
86	
87	\emph{Decoding coverage.}
88	We test four decoding strategies (greedy, nucleus $p{=}0.9$, $T{=}0.7$, $T{=}1.2$).
89	Other strategies---top-$k$ sampling, beam search, contrastive decoding---remain untested and may yield different feature importance patterns or boundary behavior.
90	
91	\emph{Operational versus theoretical boundary.}
92	$\Kempirical$ is an operational lower bound that depends on the specific feature set and classifier.
93	$\Ktheory$ may be strictly greater; a more expressive classifier or richer feature representation could push $\Kempirical$ higher.
94	
95	\emph{Feature representation.}
96	The 15-dimensional hand-crafted feature set, while interpretable and transferable, is unlikely to be optimal.
97	Learned representations---fine-tuned embeddings or probing classifiers---may capture depth-discriminative patterns that statistical features miss, potentially achieving a higher $\Kempirical$.
98	
99	\paragraph{Graduated Filtering.}
100	% [TODO: exp-006 graduated filtering results pending.
101	% Will discuss: 6 strategies × 5 seeds × α sweep results,
102	% graduated vs binary filtering comparison on MMLU/HellaSwag,
103	% practical implications of coarse 3-class ordinal signal for data curation.]
104	
```

($\\auc = 0.5139$) to rank~5 under $T{=}1.2$ ($\\auc = 0.5905$), illustrating how high-entropy decoding amplifies frequency-distribution artifacts.\n\nThis dissociation reveals an important frequency-distribution artifacts while attenuating vocabulary-diversity signals, shifting which text properties carry the most discriminative power without altering whether adjacent generations can be distinguished.\nFor practitioners, this implies that a universal depth classifier should incorporate features from multiple groups---surprisal moments, vocabulary diversity, and frequency tail statistics---to maintain performance across unknown decoding conditions.\n\nThe cross-model feature ordering (Section~\\ref{sec:feature_degradation}) provides a complementary perspective: among continuation-mode models, single-feature $\\auc$ rankings are highly consistent (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.85$), with surprisal-variance features---\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}---dominating uniformly.\nA genuine limitation, however, is that depth-pair-level agreement weakens to $\\tau \\approx 0.37$ at the 1v2 boundary (Section~\\ref{sec:feature_degradation}), indicating that the relative contribution of individual features at the discrimination boundary varies across models even when the aggregate ordering is shared.\n\n\\subsection{LLM Zero-Shot Baseline}\n\\label{sec:discuss_llm}\n\nFrontier LLMs provide a natural baseline for depth estimation.\nOn a 500-sample test set with 5 depth classes (depths 0--4), GPT-5.5 achieves 33.2\\% accuracy (MAE = 1.10) and Claude Opus~4.6 achieves 33.1\\% accuracy (MAE = 1.15)---both below the OBD-LightGBM baseline of 38.6\\% (MAE = 1.11).\nSimultaneously, both models achieve approximately 88\\% binary accuracy on the human-vs.-synthetic distinction (GPT-5.5: 87.8\\%, Claude: 88.2\\%).\n\nThis gap between binary detection and depth estimation is informative.\nFrontier LLMs can reliably determine \\emph{whether} a text is machine-generated but cannot estimate \\emph{how many generations of recursive processing} it has undergone.\nDepth estimation requires sensitivity to subtle distributional shifts between adjacent generations---shifts orders of magnitude smaller than the human-vs.-machine gap.\nThe 15-dimensional statistical feature pipeline, despite its simplicity, captures these fine-grained shifts more effectively than frontier LLMs in a zero-shot setting.\nThis motivates the dedicated feature-engineering approach: the task demands specialized statistical sensitivity rather than general language understanding.\n\n\\subsection{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nWe identify six limitations that bound the generalizability of our findings.\n\n\\emph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments use models at or below 1.5B parameters (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct).\nLarger models may produce higher-fidelity text with weaker depth signatures, potentially shifting $\\Kempirical$ beyond~2.\n\n\\emph{Prompt-chain versus retrain-chain.}\nOur recursive generation protocol is a prompt-chain: the same fixed model repeatedly generates from the previous output.\nReal-world model collapse involves retrain-chains, where models are retrained on data from earlier generations~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse}.\nRetrain-chains introduce additional degradation through training dynamics, potentially producing different $\\Kstar$ values.\n\n\\emph{Language and domain.}\nAll experiments use English text from the C4 corpus.\nDepth dynamics may differ across languages, domains (e.g., code, scientific text), and source corpora with different distributional properties.\n\n\\emph{Decoding coverage.}\nWe test four decoding strategies (greedy, nucleus $p{=}0.9$, $T{=}0.7$, $T{=}1.2$).\nOther strategies---top-$k$ sampling, beam search, contrastive decoding---remain untested and may yield different feature importance patterns or boundary behavior.\n\n\\emph{Operational versus theoretical boundary.}\n$\\Kempirical$ is an operational lower bound that depends on the specific feature set and classifier.\n$\\Ktheory$ may be strictly greater; a more expressive classifier or richer feature representation could push $\\Kempirical$ higher.\n\n\\emph{Feature representation.}\nThe 15-dimensional hand-crafted feature set, while interpretable and transferable, is unlikely to be optimal.\nLearned representations---fine-tuned embeddings or probing classifiers---may capture depth-discriminative patterns that statistical features miss, potentially achieving a higher $\\Kempirical$.\n\n\\paragraph{Graduated Filtering.}\n% [TODO: exp-006 graduated filtering results pending.\n% Will discuss: 6 strategies × 5 seeds × α sweep results,\n% graduated vs binary filtering comparison on MMLU/HellaSwag,\n% practical implications of coarse 3-class ordinal signal for data curation.]\n",
      "numLines": 104,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 104
    }
  }
}
```

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/memory.md\", \"content\": \"# Memory — gen-depth-contamination\\n\\n## 已知问题\\n\\n### right-padding (2026-05-12)\\nMVP pipeline 使用 right-padding（transformers 默认）。Director 确认对 MVP 判定无影响（所有 depth 一致受影响），不中断当前实验。**full study 阶段必须修复为 `tokenizer.padding_side = \\\"left\\\"`**，否则 batch 生成时 padding tokens 会影响生成质量的绝对水平。\\n\\n## GPU 优化建议 (2026-05-12)\\n\\n### batch size 调优\\n4090D 24GB 跑 1.5B fp16 模型仅用 3.9GB 显存 (16%)，GPU 利用率 45%。\\n- Step 3 生成：batch_size 16→**64**（保守）或 128\\n- Step 4 特征提取：feat_batch_size 8→**24**（保守）或 32\\n- 预估节省 ~74 min (38%)，加 flash-attn 可再省 10-15%\\n- full study 时一并修复\\n\\n## AutoDL 环境经验\\n\\n### C4 数据集加载 (2026-05-12)\\nAutoDL 代理下 `load_dataset(\\\"allenai/c4\\\", \\\"en\\\", streaming=True)` 会触发全量文件树解析（数千个 shard 的分页 API 请求），ProxyError 无限重试。修复：用单 shard URL 直接加载 + `HF_ENDPOINT=https://hf-mirror.com` 中转。\\n\\n### 环境变量必须固化到启动脚本 (2026-05-12)\\nAutoDL 上 `source /etc/network_turbo`、SSL 证书、HF 配置等环境变量不能只在 shell session 里 export，必须写入 `run_mvp.sh` 等包装脚本，否则 tmux session 里不生效。\\n\\n### submit_training_job pid=\\\"unknown\\\" 问题 (2026-05-13)\\nsubmit_training_job 有时返回 pid=\\\"unknown\\\"，导致 registry 状态停在 PREPARING、cron 未创建。exp-017 和 exp-003 都遇到此问题。**解决方式**：提交后必须派 Worker SSH 检查实际进程状态（tmux ls + ps aux），手动修正 registry 并注册 cron。\\n\\n### OpenRouter 代理 model ID 规则 (2026-05-13)\\n代理 `http://47.94.22.126/v1` 的 Claude model ID 不接受 `anthropic/` 前缀（返回 503 model_not_found），必须用裸 ID 如 `claude-opus-4-6`。GPT 模型用 `openai/gpt-5.5` 前缀正常。遇到新模型先用 python 单条测试多个 ID 变体再批量跑。\\n\\n### 服务器别名变更导致 cron 误标 failed (2026-05-13)\\nAutoDL 实例别名从 `autodl-4090d` 改为 `westc-26893`，cron 监控用旧名 SSH 失败 5 次后自动标 exp-003 为 failed，但实验进程从未中断。**教训**：(1) 服务器别名变更后必须立即更新所有 cron 的 server 字段；(2) cron 标 failed 时先 SSH 验证进程是否存活再信任结论；(3) registry DONE(failed) → RUNNING 需走 PREPARING→PENDING→QUEUED→RUNNING 完整链。\\n\\n## 实验设计决策\\n\\n### Generation mode 影响 depth signal 强度 (2026-05-13)\\nRewrite mode（instruction prompting）比 continuation mode 多产生 ~8.4pp AUC 差距（Qwen: 0.6957 vs 0.6114）。原因推测：instruction-following 引入更大的分布偏移，放大 depth-discriminative 信号。论文中 K* 应同时报告两种 mode 的结果；C1 robustness 依赖 continuation mode（更保守的 lower bound）。\\n\\n### Degradation ordering mode-dependent (2026-05-13)\\n4-model τ 矩阵表明 generation mode 贡献 ~60-70% 的排序偏离，架构 ~30-40%。C2 claim 不能说 \\\"universal ordering\\\"，应改为 \\\"mode-conditioned consistency with architecture residuals\\\"。Director 决策 D008 的后续：4-model τ 数据已齐全，等 Director 确认 C2 最终措辞。\\n\\n### exp-011 LLM zero-shot 结果的论文意义 (2026-05-13)\\nGPT-5.5 和 Claude Opus 4.6 在 5-class depth estimation 上均低于 OBD-LightGBM（~33% vs 38.6% acc）。但 binary human/AI detection 高达 ~88%——LLM 能分辨人写 vs AI 写，但无法判断 generational depth。这是 Introduction motivation 的核心论据：\\\"depth estimation requires specialized statistical methods, not just scale\\\"。注意 per-depth pattern 差异大：GPT-5.5 对 depth 0 敏感 (77%) 但 depth 4 近乎随机 (8%)；Claude 相反 (depth 4=37%)。\\n\", \"total_chars\": 2657, \"truncated\": false}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {}}"}

`http://47.94.22.126/v1` 的 Claude model ID 不接受 `anthropic/` 前缀（返回 503 model_not_found），必须用裸 ID 如 `claude-opus-4-6`。GPT 模型用 `openai/gpt-5.5` 前缀正常。遇到新模型先用 python 单条测试多个 ID 变体再批量跑。\\\\n\\\\n### 服务器别名变更导致 cron 误标 failed (2026-05-13)\\\\nAutoDL 实例别名从 `autodl-4090d` 改为 `westc-26893`，cron 监控用旧名 SSH 失败 5 次后自动标 exp-003 为 failed，但实验进程从未中断。**教训**：(1) 服务器别名变更后必须立即更新所有 cron 的 server 字段；(2) cron 标 failed 时先 SSH 验证进程是否存活再信任结论；(3) registry DONE(failed) → RUNNING 需走 PREPARING→PENDING→QUEUED→RUNNING 完整链。\\\\n\\\\n## 实验设计决策\\\\n\\\\n### Generation mode 影响 depth signal 强度 (2026-05-13)\\\\nRewrite mode（instruction prompting）比 continuation mode 多产生 ~8.4pp AUC 差距（Qwen: 0.6957 vs 0.6114）。原因推测：instruction-following 引入更大的分布偏移，放大 depth-discriminative 信号。论文中 K* 应同时报告两种 mode 的结果；C1 robustness 依赖 continuation mode（更保守的 lower bound）。\\\\n\\\\n### Degradation ordering mode-dependent (2026-05-13)\\\\n4-model τ 矩阵表明 generation mode 贡献 ~60-70% 的排序偏离，架构 ~30-40%。C2 claim 不能说 \\\\\\\"universal ordering\\\\\\\"，应改为 \\\\\\\"mode-conditioned consistency with architecture residuals\\\\\\\"。Director 决策 D008 的后续：4-model τ 数据已齐全，等 Director 确认 C2 最终措辞。\\\\n\\\\n### exp-011 LLM zero-shot 结果的论文意义 (2026-05-13)\\\\nGPT-5.5 和 Claude Opus 4.6 在 5-class depth estimation 上均低于 OBD-LightGBM（~33% vs 38.6% acc）。但 binary human/AI detection 高达 ~88%——LLM 能分辨人写 vs AI 写，但无法判断 generational depth。这是 Introduction motivation 的核心论据：\\\\\\\"depth estimation requires specialized statistical methods, not just scale\\\\\\\"。注意 per-depth pattern 差异大：GPT-5.5 对 depth 0 敏感 (77%) 但 depth 4 近乎随机 (8%)；Claude 相反 (depth 1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate generational depth estimation on four model configurations:
5	Pythia-1.4B and OLMo-1B in continuation mode, and Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct in both rewrite and continuation modes.
6	All experiments use the OBD-LightGBM classifier with the 15-dimensional feature set described in Section~\ref{sec:method}.
7	Each configuration uses a balanced corpus of 5{,}000 texts per depth level (generations 0--5).
8	We organize results around four questions:
9	(1)~Does $\Kempirical = 2$ hold across configurations?
10	(2)~Do depth classifiers transfer across models?
11	(3)~Is the feature degradation ordering model-independent?
12	(4)~How does generation mode affect detectability?
13

19	across all four configurations, with bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals
20	(1{,}000 resamples) and permutation-test $p$-values for the critical 1v2
21	and 2v3 boundaries.

61	\bottomrule
62	\end{tabular}
63	\end{table}
64	
65	The pattern is uniform across all four configurations:
66	0v1 discrimination is near-perfect ($\auc > 0.99$),
67	followed by a sharp drop at 1v2 ($\auc$ range: 0.6112--0.6955),
68	with 2v3 falling below the decision threshold $\theta = 0.60$
69	($\auc$ range: 0.5496--0.5893).
70	All subsequent pairs (3v4, 4v5) converge toward chance.
71	Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} visualizes this decay.
72	
73	\begin{figure}[t]
74	\centering
75	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_auc_decay.pdf}
76	\caption{Pairwise $\auc$ as a function of depth pair across four model-mode configurations (solid) and four decoding strategies (dashed).  All configurations show a sharp drop between 0v1 and 1v2, with 2v3 and deeper pairs falling below the decision threshold $\theta = 0.60$ (horizontal dashed line).  Error bars show bootstrap 95\% CIs for model-mode configurations; decoding strategy curves omit CIs as they were computed on a separate experimental setup.}
77	\label{fig:auc_decay}
78	\end{figure}
79	
80	For the $\Kempirical$ determination, all four 1v2 CI lower bounds exceed
81	$\theta$: Qwen-rw ($\text{CI}_\text{low} = 0.6859$), Pythia (0.6454),
82	OLMo (0.6045), and Qwen-cont (0.6015).
83	Conversely, all four 2v3 CI upper bounds fall below $\theta$:
84	Qwen-rw ($\text{CI}_\text{high} = 0.5991$), Qwen-cont (0.5712),
85	Pythia (0.5628), and OLMo (0.5596).
86	Permutation tests confirm $p = 0.002$ for all eight boundary comparisons.
87	Together these establish $\Kempirical = 2$ as a consistent discrimination boundary across all tested configurations at $\leq$1.5B scale, suggesting universality beyond the specific models studied.
88	
89	The most marginal case is Qwen-cont, where the 1v2 CI lower bound
90	(0.6015) exceeds $\theta$ by only 0.0015.  This narrow margin
91	indicates that $\Kempirical = 2$ is robust but not trivially so---continuation-mode
92	generation produces weaker depth artifacts than rewrite mode, pushing the
93	boundary closer to the detection threshold.
94	
95	The absolute 1v2 $\auc$ varies across models:
96	Qwen-rw (0.6955) $>$ Pythia (0.6539) $>$ OLMo (0.6145) $\approx$ Qwen-cont (0.6112).
97	Notably, the spread due to generation mode within a single model
98	(Qwen-rw vs.\ Qwen-cont: $\Delta = 8.4$\,pp) exceeds the spread
99	across models in the same mode
100	(Pythia vs.\ OLMo: $\Delta = 3.9$\,pp),
101	suggesting that generation protocol is a stronger determinant
102	of detectability than model identity.
103	We examine both factors in Sections~\ref{sec:transfer}
104	and~\ref{sec:generation_mode}.
105	
106	Beyond the $\Kempirical$ boundary, depth discrimination deteriorates
107	rapidly.
108	OLMo shows nonsignificant permutation $p$-values at 3v4
109	($p = 0.064$) and 4v5 ($p = 0.232$), indicating that depth
110	classification beyond generation~2 is indistinguishable from
111	random for this model.
112	Pythia retains marginal effects at these depths
113	($p = 0.006$ and $p = 0.008$, respectively),
114	while Qwen-cont shows weak but detectable discrimination through
115	3v4 ($p = 0.002$) that fades at 4v5 ($p = 0.006$).
116	
117	%% ====================================================================
118	\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}
119	\label{sec:transfer}
120	
121	Having established $\Kempirical = 2$ consistently, we next ask: is this boundary a property of the text itself, or an artifact of model self-familiarity?
122	To answer this, we train classifiers on one model's outputs and evaluate on another's.
123	Table~\ref{tab:transfer_matrix} shows the full
124	$4 \times 4$ transfer accuracy matrix for 6-class ordinal depth
125	classification.
126	
127	\begin{table}[t]
128	\centering
129	\small
130	\caption{Cross-model transfer accuracy (6-class ordinal
131	classification).  Diagonal entries (bold): within-model accuracy.
132	Off-diagonal: cross-model transfer.  Retention is computed as
133	off-diagonal accuracy divided by the diagonal entry of the training
134	model.}
135	\label{tab:transfer_matrix}
136	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
137	\toprule
138	Train $\downarrow$ / Test $\rightarrow$
139	  & Qwen-rw & Pythia & OLMo & Qwen-cont \\
140	\midrule
141	Qwen-rw
142	  & \textbf{0.4229} & 0.2590 & 0.2653 & 0.3466 \\
143	Pythia
144	  & 0.2718 & \textbf{0.3856} & 0.3531 & 0.3287 \\
145	OLMo
146	  & 0.2768 & 0.3450 & \textbf{0.3822} & 0.3244 \\
147	Qwen-cont
148	  & 0.3440 & 0.3284 & 0.3112 & \textbf{0.3875} \\
149	\bottomrule
150	\end{tabular}
151	\end{table}
152	
153	\begin{figure}[t]
154	\centering
155	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_transfer_heatmap.pdf}
156	\caption{Cross-model transfer heatmap (6-class ordinal accuracy).
157	Rows: training model; columns: test model.
158	Pythia$\leftrightarrow$OLMo (both continuation mode) shows high
159	mutual transfer (90--92\% accuracy retention), while Qwen-rewrite
160	transfers poorly to continuation-mode models (61--63\%).
161	Diagonal entries are within-model baselines.}
162	\label{fig:transfer_heatmap}
163	\end{figure}
164	
165	Two principal findings emerge (Figure~\ref{fig:transfer_heatmap}).
166	First, transfer between continuation-mode models is high.
167	Pythia$\to$OLMo retains 90--92\% of within-model accuracy, and
168	the reverse direction is comparable
169	(85--86\% retention by Kendall $\tau$).
170	The accuracy-based retention of 90--92\% is complemented by the
171	$\tau$-based retention of 85--86\%, indicating that not only
172	classification accuracy but also the relative depth ordering of
173	predictions is largely preserved.
174	Qwen-cont$\leftrightarrow$Pythia also transfers well
175	(84--85\% accuracy retention),
176	and Qwen-cont$\leftrightarrow$OLMo achieves 80--85\%.
177	These retention rates---80--92\% of within-model accuracy preserved across continuation-mode pairs---suggest that a large component of the depth signal is text-intrinsic rather than tied to a specific generator.
178	
179	Second, Qwen-rewrite exhibits catastrophic transfer failure.
180	Classifiers trained on Qwen-rewrite retain only 61--63\% of
181	within-model accuracy when evaluated on Pythia or OLMo
182	(45--50\% by $\tau$).
183	The reverse direction transfers asymmetrically: Pythia/OLMo${\to}$Qwen-rw retains 70--72\% of within-model accuracy, higher than the forward 61--63\% but still far below continuation-mode transfer rates (80--92\%).
184	This confirms that rewrite-mode classifiers rely on generation
185	artifacts that do not generalize across models, in contrast
186	to Qwen-cont, which achieves 80--85\% retention to the same targets.
187	
188	At the 0v1 boundary, cross-model pairwise $\auc$ exceeds 0.96
189	for all model pairs, confirming that the human-vs.-synthetic
190	distinction is universally transferable regardless of generator
191	or mode.
192	The transfer degradation concentrates at deeper boundaries
193	(1v2 and beyond), where model- and mode-specific factors dominate.
194	
195	The aggregate picture is one of modest cross-model loss:
196	mean pairwise $\auc$ is 0.6099 within-model versus 0.5905
197	cross-model.
198	This gap is driven primarily by Qwen-rewrite; among
199	continuation-mode pairs the within/cross difference narrows
200	further.
201	
202	%% ====================================================================
203	\subsection{Feature Degradation Ordering}
204	\label{sec:feature_degradation}
205	
206	The transfer results indicate that depth signals are largely text-intrinsic.
207	We next ask what drives discrimination: if the signal is text-intrinsic, different generators should rely on the same features in the same order.
208	We test this by computing single-feature $\auc$ rankings
209	independently for each model and comparing them via rank
210	correlation.
211	
212	\begin{table}[t]
213	\centering
214	\small
215	\caption{Top-3 features by single-feature $\auc$ ranking for each
216	model configuration.
217	Features shared across all three continuation-mode models are in bold.
218	Qwen-rewrite's top feature (\texttt{freq\_entropy}) is mode-specific.
219	Spearman $\rho$ (continuation-mode pairs): Pythia--OLMo 0.95,
220	Pythia--Qwen-cont 0.9143, OLMo--Qwen-cont 0.85.}
221	\label{tab:top_features}
222	\begin{tabular}{cllll}
223	\toprule
224	Rank & Pythia & OLMo & Qwen-rw & Qwen-cont \\
225	\midrule
226	1
227	  & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}}
228	  & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_std}}
229	  & \texttt{freq\_entropy}
230	  & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}} \\
231	2
232	  & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d2\_std}}
233	  & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}}
234	  & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_std}}
235	  & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_std}} \\
236	3
237	  & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_std}}
238	  & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d2\_std}}
239	  & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}}
240	  & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d2\_std}} \\
241	\bottomrule
242	\end{tabular}
243	\end{table}
244	
245	Among continuation-mode models, single-feature $\auc$ rankings
246	are highly correlated:
247	Pythia vs.\ OLMo $\rho = 0.95$,
248	Pythia vs.\ Qwen-cont $\rho = 0.9143$,
249	OLMo vs.\ Qwen-cont $\rho = 0.85$
250	(all $\rho \geq 0.85$).
251	The top-3 features---\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}, \texttt{surp\_std},
252	and \texttt{surp\_d2\_std}---are shared (up to ordering) across
253	all three continuation-mode configurations
254	(Table~\ref{tab:top_features}).
255	All three measure variability of surprisal or its derivatives,
256	capturing the \emph{heterogeneity} of token-level predictability
257	across the text.
258	That these features degrade in a shared order across generators
259	suggests a common mechanism: each generation step smooths the
260	token-level surprisal landscape, reducing its variance in a
261	model-independent fashion.
262	
263	Qwen-rewrite diverges: its top-ranked feature is
264	\texttt{freq\_entropy}, a lexical diversity measure absent from
265	the continuation-mode top-3 for any model.
266	This corroborates the transfer results in
267	Section~\ref{sec:transfer}---rewrite-mode classifiers learn
268	partly from instruction-following artifacts rather than the
269	shared surprisal-based signals.
270	
271	Kendall $\tau$ provides a complementary, more conservative
272	perspective:
273	Pythia vs.\ OLMo $\tau = 0.733$,
274	Pythia vs.\ Qwen-cont $\tau = 0.695$,
275	OLMo vs.\ Qwen-cont $\tau = 0.543$.
276	Cross-mode correlations are lower:
277	Pythia vs.\ Qwen-rw $\tau = 0.467$,
278	OLMo vs.\ Qwen-rw $\tau = 0.429$.
279	
280	\paragraph{Limitation.}
281	The high Spearman $\rho$ characterizes the \emph{aggregate}
282	feature ranking, but at the depth-pair level the agreement
283	weakens: $\tau \approx 0.37$ at the 1v2 boundary.
284	This means that while the overall importance ordering is shared,
285	the relative contribution of individual features at the
286	discrimination boundary---where precision matters most---varies
287	across models.
288	We view this as a genuine limitation of the text-intrinsic
289	interpretation and a direction for future investigation.
290	
291	%% ====================================================================
292	\subsection{Generation Mode Effect}
293	\label{sec:generation_mode}
294	
295	Finally, we ask whether generation mode---an experimental design choice rather than a text property---affects depth detectability.
296	Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct provides a controlled comparison: rewrite (instruction-based paraphrasing) and continuation (prompt truncation) share the same model weights, as defined in Section~\ref{sec:method}, isolating the effect of generation protocol.
297	
298	Rewrite mode yields higher 1v2 $\auc$ than continuation:
299	0.6955 vs.\ 0.6112, a difference of $+$8.4 percentage points.
300	The advantage persists at 2v3
301	(0.5893 vs.\ 0.5609, $\Delta = +$2.8\,pp)
302	and narrows at 3v4
303	(0.5435 vs.\ 0.5409, $\Delta = +$0.3\,pp),
304	with both modes converging toward chance at deeper levels.
305	The amplified detectability under rewrite mode is consistent
306	with instruction-following introducing systematic deviations
307	from the source text's style---additional artifacts that are
308	discriminable but mode-specific rather than text-intrinsic.
309	
310	Transfer between modes is asymmetric.
311	A classifier trained on Qwen-cont and evaluated on Qwen-rw
312	retains 89\% of within-model accuracy
313	(0.3440 vs.\ 0.3875 diagonal),
314	whereas the reverse direction---Qwen-rw trained,
315	Qwen-cont tested---retains 82\%
316	(0.3466 vs.\ 0.4229 diagonal).
317	The direction of the asymmetry is intuitive:
318	continuation-mode signals form a subset of the information
319	available in rewrite-mode data
320	(which adds instruction-following artifacts on top),
321	so a continuation-trained classifier can partially recognize
322	rewrite-mode outputs but not vice versa.
323	
324	This asymmetry is mirrored in the feature rankings.
325	Qwen-rewrite's top feature (\texttt{freq\_entropy}) is
326	mode-specific and ranks outside the top-3 for every
327	continuation-mode model.
328	Qwen-cont's top feature (\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}) is shared
329	with Pythia and OLMo, confirming that continuation mode
330	captures primarily text-intrinsic depth signals.
331	
332	The practical implication is a detectability--generalizability
333	tradeoff.
334	Rewrite-mode classifiers achieve higher absolute $\auc$ but
335	transfer poorly across models
336	(Qwen-rw$\to$Pythia/OLMo: 61--63\% retention).
337	Continuation-mode classifiers sacrifice 8.4\,pp of within-model
338	$\auc$ but achieve robust cross-model transfer
339	(Qwen-cont$\to$Pythia: 84--85\%).
340	For deployment scenarios requiring model-agnostic depth
341	estimation, continuation-mode features provide a more reliable
342	foundation despite lower raw discriminability.
343	
```

</details>

\\textbf{\\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAmong continuation-mode models, single-feature $\\auc$ rankings\nare highly correlated:\nPythia vs.\\ OLMo $\\rho = 0.95$,\nPythia vs.\\ Qwen-cont $\\rho = 0.9143$,\nOLMo vs.\\ Qwen-cont $\\rho = 0.85$\n(all $\\rho \\geq 0.85$).\nThe top-3 features---\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std},\nand \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}---are shared (up to ordering) across\nall three continuation-mode configurations\n(Table~\\ref{tab:top_features}).\nAll three measure variability of surprisal or its derivatives,\ncapturing the \\emph{heterogeneity} of token-level predictability\nacross the text.\nThat these features degrade in a shared order across generators\nsuggests a common mechanism: each generation step smooths the\ntoken-level surprisal landscape, reducing its variance in a\nmodel-independent fashion.\n\nQwen-rewrite diverges: its top-ranked feature is\n\\texttt{freq\\_entropy}, a lexical diversity measure absent from\nthe continuation-mode top-3 for any model.\nThis corroborates the transfer results in\nSection~\\ref{sec:transfer}---rewrite-mode classifiers learn\npartly from instruction-following artifacts rather than the\nshared surprisal-based signals.\n\nKendall $\\tau$ provides a complementary, more conservative\nperspective:\nPythia vs.\\ OLMo $\\tau = 0.733$,\nPythia vs.\\ Qwen-cont $\\tau = 0.695$,\nOLMo vs.\\ Qwen-cont $\\tau = 0.543$.\nCross-mode correlations are lower:\nPythia vs.\\ Qwen-rw $\\tau = 0.467$,\nOLMo vs.\\ Qwen-rw $\\tau = 0.429$.\n\n\\paragraph{Limitation.}\nThe high Spearman $\\rho$ characterizes the \\emph{aggregate}\nfeature ranking, but at the depth-pair level the agreement\nweakens: $\\tau \\approx 0.37$ at the 1v2 boundary.\nThis means that while the overall importance ordering is shared,\nthe relative contribution of individual features at the\ndiscrimination boundary---where precision matters most---varies\nacross models.\nWe view this as a genuine limitation of the text-intrinsic\ninterpretation and a direction for future investigation.\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Generation Mode Effect}\n\\label{sec:generation_mode}\n\nFinally, we ask whether generation mode---an experimental design choice rather than a text property---affects depth detectability.\nQwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct provides a controlled comparison: rewrite (instruction-based paraphrasing) and continuation (prompt truncation) share the same model weights, as defined in Section~\\ref{sec:method}, isolating the effect of generation protocol.\n\nRewrite mode yields higher 1v2 $\\auc$ than continuation:\n0.6955 vs.\\ 0.6112, a difference of $+$8.4 percentage points.\nThe advantage persists at 2v3\n(0.5893 vs.\\ 0.5609, $\\Delta = +$2.8\\,pp)\nand narrows at 3v4\n(0.5435 vs.\\ 0.5409, $\\Delta = +$0.3\\,pp),\nwith both modes converging toward chance at deeper levels.\nThe amplified detectability under rewrite mode is consistent\nwith instruction-following introducing systematic deviations\nfrom the source text's style---additional artifacts that are\ndiscriminable but mode-specific rather than text-intrinsic.\n\nTransfer between modes is asymmetric.\nA classifier trained on Qwen-cont and evaluated on Qwen-rw\nretains 89\\% of within-model accuracy\n(0.3440 vs.\\ 0.3875 diagonal),\nwhereas the reverse direction---Qwen-rw trained,\nQwen-cont tested---retains 82\\%\n(0.3466 vs.\\ 0.4229 diagonal).\nThe direction of the asymmetry is intuitive:\ncontinuation-mode signals form a subset of the information\navailable in rewrite-mode data\n(which adds instruction-following artifacts on top),\nso a continuation-trained classifier can partially recognize\nrewrite-mode outputs but not vice versa.\n\nThis asymmetry is mirrored in the feature rankings.\nQwen-rewrite's top feature (\\texttt{freq\\_entropy}) is\nmode-specific and ranks outside the top-3 for every\ncontinuation-mode model.\nQwen-cont's top feature (\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}) is shared\nwith Pythia and OLMo, confirming that continuation mode\ncaptures primarily text-intrinsic depth signals.\n\nThe practical implication is a detectability--generalizability\ntradeoff.\nRewrite-mode classifiers achieve higher absolute $\\auc$ but\ntransfer poorly across models\n(Qwen-rw$\\to$Pythia/OLMo: 61--63\\% retention).\nContinuation-mode classifiers sacrifice 8.4\\,pp of within-model\n$\\auc$ but achieve robust cross-model transfer\n(Qwen-cont$\\to$Pythia: 84--85\\%).\nFor deployment scenarios requiring model-agnostic depth\nestimation, continuation-mode features provide a more reliable\nfoundation despite lower raw discriminability.\n",

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We presented the first systematic measurement of the generational depth discrimination boundary $\Kempirical$ for recursive synthetic text.
5	Across 8 configurations spanning 4 model-mode combinations (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, and Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct in continuation mode, with Qwen additionally in rewrite mode) and 4 decoding strategies, we establish $\Kempirical = 2$ as a consistent discrimination boundary: classifiers can reliably distinguish adjacent generations up to depth~2, but beyond this boundary adjacent depths are operationally indistinguishable ($\auc$ below $\theta = 0.60$).
6	This consistency across all 8 tested conditions suggests universality beyond the $\leq$1.5B scale studied.
7	The 0v1 boundary is near-perfectly separable ($\auc > 0.99$), followed by a sharp drop of approximately 30 percentage points at 1v2 ($\auc$: 0.6112--0.6955).
8	Cross-model classifiers retain 80--92\% of within-model accuracy among continuation-mode models, with 0v1 transfer $\auc$ exceeding 0.96 universally---indicating that depth signals are largely text-intrinsic.
9	Feature degradation orderings are highly consistent across model families (Spearman $\rho \geq 0.85$), with surprisal-variance features dominating uniformly.
10	The boundary is invariant to decoding strategy, though feature importance rankings diverge between low- and high-entropy sampling ($\rho = 0.914$ for greedy vs.\ $T{=}0.7$; $\rho = 0.53$ for greedy vs.\ $T{=}1.2$).
11	Frontier LLMs achieve approximately 88\% binary human-vs.-synthetic accuracy but only 33\% on 5-class depth estimation, below the OBD-LightGBM baseline of 38.6\%, confirming that depth estimation is a distinct and harder task than binary detection.
12	
13	% [TODO: graduated filtering conclusion pending exp-006 results.
14	% Will summarize: whether coarse 3-class ordinal signal (human / shallow / deep)
15	% improves downstream task performance over binary filtering on MMLU/HellaSwag.]
16	
17	Our results are bounded by the scale of models tested ($\leq$1.5B parameters), the use of prompt-chain rather than retrain-chain protocols, and restriction to English text from the C4 corpus.
18	The 15-dimensional hand-crafted feature set yields $\Kempirical$ as an operational lower bound; $\Ktheory$ may be strictly higher.
19	The boundary determination is bilateral: the most marginal inclusion is Qwen-cont ($\text{CI}_\text{lower} = 0.6015$, exceeding $\theta$ by 0.0015), while the most marginal exclusion is Qwen-rw at 2v3 ($\text{CI}_\text{upper} = 0.5991$, falling below $\theta$ by only 0.0009).
20	Depth-pair-level feature agreement also weakens at the boundary ($\tau \approx 0.37$ at 1v2).
21	
22	Several directions merit future investigation.
23	Validating $\Kempirical$ under retrain-chain protocols---where models are iteratively trained on synthetic data from previous generations---would establish whether the prompt-chain boundary generalizes to the model collapse setting.
24	Scaling to larger models (7B+ and 70B+) would test whether increased generation fidelity shifts $\Kempirical$.
25	Extending to multilingual and multi-domain corpora would assess the generality of the surprisal-based degradation mechanism.
26	Tracking temporal dynamics of web contamination depth in the wild would connect our controlled measurements to real-world corpus evolution.
27	Finally, learned representations may achieve higher $\Kempirical$ than hand-crafted features, narrowing the gap between $\Kempirical$ and $\Ktheory$.
28	
```

  "new_string": "Recursive generation thus acts as a severely lossy channel whose information bottleneck is traversed within two steps.\n\nThe feature degradation analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:feature_degradation}) provides mechanistic evidence for this rapid collapse.\nThe most discriminative features---surprisal variance (\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std}) and its derivatives---all capture the heterogeneity of token-level predictability.\nHuman text exhibits high surprisal variance reflecting diverse syntactic structures and unpredictable discourse patterns.\nThe first generation step smooths this variance substantially, replacing high-surprisal tokens and atypical constructions with more predictable alternatives; the second step reduces the remaining variance to near-floor levels, leaving insufficient statistical fingerprint for reliable adjacent-depth discrimination.\nCrucially, this degradation ordering is consistent across model families (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.85$ for all continuation-mode pairs), suggesting that the two-step information loss is governed by the autoregressive generation mechanism itself rather than by model-specific biases.\n\nThis pattern aligns with findings from the model collapse literature."

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Theoretical Implications of $\\Kempirical = 2$}\n\\label{sec:discuss_kstar}\n\nThe Data Processing Inequality guarantees the existence of a finite $\\Ktheory$ beyond which adjacent generations become indistinguishable (Section~\\ref{sec:formulation}).\nOur empirical result---$\\Kempirical = 2$ uniformly across all 8 tested configurations (4 model-mode combinations from Section~\\ref{sec:kstar_boundary} and 4 decoding strategies from the ablation)---establishes that this boundary is not only finite but remarkably shallow at the $\\leq$1.5B parameter scale.\n\nThe shallowness of $\\Kempirical$ is explained by the rate of information loss across generations.\nThe 0v1 boundary is near-trivially separable ($\\auc > 0.99$ across all configurations), reflecting the large distributional gap between human text and first-generation synthetic text.\nAt the 1v2 boundary, $\\auc$ drops by approximately 30 percentage points ($\\auc$ range: 0.6112--0.6955), indicating that the majority of the discriminative signal is consumed in the first generation step.\nBy the 2v3 boundary, $\\auc$ falls to 0.5496--0.5893---below the operational threshold $\\theta = 0.60$---and deeper pairs converge toward chance.\nRecursive generation thus acts as a severely lossy channel whose information bottleneck is traversed within two steps.\n\nThis pattern aligns with findings from the model collapse literature.\nShumailov et al.~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse} observed that tail-distribution degradation is most acute in the earliest generations, with minority modes vanishing before the output distribution stabilizes.\nDohmatob et al.~\\cite{dohmatob2024model} derived scaling laws demonstrating that tail probability mass is lost at rates predictable from model and data scale.\nOur $\\Kempirical = 2$ provides a complementary, classifier-based measurement of this phenomenon: the information-theoretic discrimination horizon is reached after just two generation steps.\nAlemohammad et al.~\\cite{alemohammad2024selfconsuming} proved convergence to a fixed point for self-consuming generative models; $\\Kempirical = 2$ indicates that, operationally, the approach to this fixed point is rapid enough that adjacent-generation distinctions collapse within two steps.\n\nThe invariance of $\\Kempirical = 2$ across decoding strategies merits emphasis.\nGreedy decoding, nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.9$), $T{=}0.7$, and $T{=}1.2$ all yield $\\Kempirical = 2$, with 1v2 $\\auc$ spanning a narrow range of [0.6866, 0.7039].\nHigher decoding entropy modestly increases signal strength ($\\auc_{1,2}$: 0.69 greedy $\\to$ 0.70 at $T{=}1.2$) but does not shift the boundary.\nThis robustness suggests that $\\Kempirical$ is governed by the fundamental information bottleneck of the generation process rather than by surface-level properties of the sampling algorithm.\n\n\\subsection{Implications of Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:discuss_transfer}\n\nThe cross-model transfer results (Section~\\ref{sec:transfer}) indicate that depth signals are largely encoded in text-level statistical properties rather than generator-specific artifacts.\nClassifiers trained on Pythia-1.4B and evaluated on OLMo-1B retain 90--92\\% of within-model accuracy, and the broader continuation-mode transfer matrix shows 80--92\\% retention across all model pairs.\nAt the 0v1 boundary, cross-model $\\auc$ exceeds 0.96 universally, confirming that binary contamination detection is fully transferable across generators.\n\nThe practical implication is direct: a single depth classifier trained on one model's outputs can be deployed to estimate generational depth for texts produced by other models, without model-specific retraining.\nThis transferability extends the utility of depth estimation to open-web settings where the generating model is unknown---precisely the scenario where depth-aware data curation is most needed.\n\nTwo caveats bound this conclusion.\nFirst, transfer retention of 80--92\\% suggests a large text-intrinsic component but does not quantify its exact share; we refrain from decomposing the signal into text-intrinsic and model-specific fractions.\nSecond, Qwen-cont$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo retention (80--85\\%) is lower than Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo (90--92\\%), indicating that model family similarity modulates transfer even among continuation-mode configurations.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Importance Across Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:discuss_features}\n\nWhile $\\Kempirical = 2$ is invariant to decoding strategy (4/4 configurations), the feature importance ordering is not uniformly consistent.\nGreedy decoding and $T{=}0.7$ share highly correlated feature rankings (Spearman $\\rho = 0.914$), with top features dominated by vocabulary diversity measures: \\texttt{self\\_bleu}, \\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}, and \\texttt{ttr}.\nIn contrast, greedy versus $T{=}1.2$ yields a lower correlation ($\\rho = 0.53$, $p = 0.041$; suggestive but nonsignificant after Bonferroni correction for 6 pairwise comparisons), with $T{=}1.2$ promoting \\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}, \\texttt{ttr}, and \\texttt{surp\\_skew} to the top ranks.\nNotably, \\texttt{freq\\_entropy} rises from rank~10 under greedy ($\\auc = 0.5139$) to rank~5 under $T{=}1.2$ ($\\auc = 0.5905$), illustrating how high-entropy decoding amplifies frequency-distribution artifacts.\n\nThis dissociation reveals an important distinction: the \\emph{existence} of the depth boundary is robust, but the \\emph{optimal feature subset} for detecting it depends on the sampling strategy.\nHigh-entropy decoding amplifies frequency-distribution artifacts while attenuating vocabulary-diversity signals, shifting which text properties carry the most discriminative power without altering whether adjacent generations can be distinguished.\nFor practitioners, this implies that a universal depth classifier should incorporate features from multiple groups---surprisal moments, vocabulary diversity, and frequency tail statistics---to maintain performance across unknown decoding conditions.\n\nThe cross-model feature ordering (Section~\\ref{sec:feature_degradation}) provides a complementary perspective: among continuation-mode models, single-feature $\\auc$ rankings are highly consistent (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.85$), with surprisal-variance features---\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}---dominating uniformly.\nA genuine limitation, however, is that depth-pair-level agreement weakens to $\\tau \\approx 0.37$ at the 1v2 boundary (Section~\\ref{sec:feature_degradation}), indicating that the relative contribution of individual features at the discrimination boundary varies across models even when the aggregate ordering is shared.\n\n\\subsection{LLM Zero-Shot Baseline}\n\\label{sec:discuss_llm}\n\nFrontier LLMs provide a natural baseline for depth estimation.\nOn a 500-sample test set with 5 depth classes (depths 0--4), GPT-5.5 achieves 33.2\\% accuracy (MAE = 1.10) and Claude Opus~4.6 achieves 33.1\\% accuracy (MAE = 1.15)---both below the OBD-LightGBM baseline of 38.6\\% (MAE = 1.11).\nSimultaneously, both models achieve approximately 88\\% binary accuracy on the human-vs.-synthetic distinction (GPT-5.5: 87.8\\%, Claude: 88.2\\%).\n\nThis gap between binary detection and depth estimation is informative.\nFrontier LLMs can reliably determine \\emph{whether} a text is machine-generated but cannot estimate \\emph{how many generations of recursive processing} it has undergone.\nDepth estimation requires sensitivity to subtle distributional shifts between adjacent generations---shifts orders of magnitude smaller than the human-vs.-machine gap.\nThe 15-dimensional statistical feature pipeline, despite its simplicity, captures these fine-grained shifts more effectively than frontier LLMs in a zero-shot setting.\nThis motivates the dedicated feature-engineering approach: the task demands specialized statistical sensitivity rather than general language understanding.\n\n\\subsection{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nWe identify six limitations that bound the generalizability of our findings.\n\n\\emph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments use models at or below 1.5B parameters (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct).\nLarger models may produce higher-fidelity text with weaker depth signatures, potentially shifting $\\Kempirical$ beyond~2.\n\n\\emph{Prompt-chain versus retrain-chain.}\nOur recursive generation protocol is a prompt-chain: the same fixed model repeatedly generates from the previous output.\nReal-world model collapse involves retrain-chains, where models are retrained on data from earlier generations~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse}.\nRetrain-chains introduce additional degradation through training dynamics, potentially producing different $\\Kstar$ values.\n\n\\emph{Language and domain.}\nAll experiments use English text from the C4 corpus.\nDepth dynamics may differ across languages, domains (e.g., code, scientific text), and source corpora with different distributional properties.\n\n\\emph{Decoding coverage.}\nWe test four decoding strategies (greedy, nucleus $p{=}0.9$, $T{=}0.7$, $T{=}1.2$).\nOther strategies---top-$k$ sampling, beam search, contrastive decoding---remain untested and may yield different feature importance patterns or boundary behavior.\n\n\\emph{Operational versus theoretical boundary.}\n$\\Kempirical$ is an operational lower bound that depends on the specific feature set and classifier.\n$\\Ktheory$ may be strictly greater; a more expressive classifier or richer feature representation could push $\\Kempirical$ higher.\n\n\\emph{Feature representation.}\nThe 15-dimensional hand-crafted feature set, while interpretable and transferable, is unlikely to be optimal.\nLearned representations---fine-tuned embeddings or probing classifiers---may capture depth-discriminative patterns that statistical features miss, potentially achieving a higher $\\Kempirical$.\n\n\\paragraph{Graduated Filtering.}\n% [TODO: exp-006 graduated filtering results pending.\n% Will discuss: 6 strategies × 5 seeds × α sweep results,\n% graduated vs binary filtering comparison on MMLU/HellaSwag,\n% practical implications of coarse 3-class ordinal signal for data curation.]\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Theoretical Implications of $\\Kempirical = 2$}\n\\label{sec:discuss_kstar}\n\nThe Data Processing Inequality guarantees the existence of a finite $\\Ktheory$ beyond which adjacent generations become indistinguishable (Section~\\ref{sec:formulation}).\nOur empirical result---$\\Kempirical = 2$ uniformly across all 8 tested configurations (4 model-mode combinations from Section~\\ref{sec:kstar_boundary} and 4 decoding strategies from the ablation)---establishes that this boundary is not only finite but remarkably shallow at the $\\leq$1.5B parameter scale.\n\nThe shallowness of $\\Kempirical$ is explained by the rate of information loss across generations.\nThe 0v1 boundary is near-trivially separable ($\\auc > 0.99$ across all configurations), reflecting the large distributional gap between human text and first-generation synthetic text.\nAt the 1v2 boundary, $\\auc$ drops by approximately 30 percentage points ($\\auc$ range: 0.6112--0.6955), indicating that the majority of the discriminative signal is consumed in the first generation step.\nBy the 2v3 boundary, $\\auc$ falls to 0.5496--0.5893---below the operational threshold $\\theta = 0.60$---and deeper pairs converge toward chance.\nRecursive generation thus acts as a severely lossy channel whose information bottleneck is traversed within two steps.\n\nThe feature degradation analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:feature_degradation}) provides mechanistic evidence for this rapid collapse.\nThe most discriminative features---surprisal variance (\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std}) and its derivatives---all capture the heterogeneity of token-level predictability.\nHuman text exhibits high surprisal variance reflecting diverse syntactic structures and unpredictable discourse patterns.\nThe first generation step smooths this variance substantially, replacing high-surprisal tokens and atypical constructions with more predictable alternatives; the second step reduces the remaining variance to near-floor levels, leaving insufficient statistical fingerprint for reliable adjacent-depth discrimination.\nCrucially, this degradation ordering is consistent across model families (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.85$ for all continuation-mode pairs), suggesting that the two-step information loss is governed by the autoregressive generation mechanism itself rather than by model-specific biases.\n\nThis pattern aligns with findings from the model collapse literature.\nShumailov et al.~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse} observed that tail-distribution degradation is most acute in the earliest generations, with minority modes vanishing before the output distribution stabilizes.\nDohmatob et al.~\\cite{dohmatob2024model} derived scaling laws demonstrating that tail probability mass is lost at rates predictable from model and data scale.\nOur $\\Kempirical = 2$ provides a complementary, classifier-based measurement of this phenomenon: the information-theoretic discrimination horizon is reached after just two generation steps.\nAlemohammad et al.~\\cite{alemohammad2024selfconsuming} proved convergence to a fixed point for self-consuming generative models; $\\Kempirical = 2$ indicates that, operationally, the approach to this fixed point is rapid enough that adjacent-generation distinctions collapse within two steps.\n\nThe invariance of $\\Kempirical = 2$ across decoding strategies merits emphasis.\nGreedy decoding, nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.9$), $T{=}0.7$, and $T{=}1.2$ all yield $\\Kempirical = 2$, with 1v2 $\\auc$ spanning a narrow range of [0.6866, 0.7039].\nHigher decoding entropy modestly increases signal strength ($\\auc_{1,2}$: 0.69 greedy $\\to$ 0.70 at $T{=}1.2$) but does not shift the boundary.\nThis robustness suggests that $\\Kempirical$ is governed by the fundamental information bottleneck of the generation process rather than by surface-level properties of the sampling algorithm.\n\n\\subsection{Implications of Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:discuss_transfer}\n\nThe cross-model transfer results (Section~\\ref{sec:transfer}) indicate that depth signals are largely encoded in text-level statistical properties rather than generator-specific artifacts.\nClassifiers trained on Pythia-1.4B and evaluated on OLMo-1B retain 90--92\\% of within-model accuracy, and the broader continuation-mode transfer matrix shows 80--92\\% retention across all model pairs.\nAt the 0v1 boundary, cross-model $\\auc$ exceeds 0.96 universally, confirming that binary contamination detection is fully transferable across generators.\n\nThe practical implication is direct: a single depth classifier trained on one model's outputs can be deployed to estimate generational depth for texts produced by other models, without model-specific retraining.\nThis transferability extends the utility of depth estimation to open-web settings where the generating model is unknown---precisely the scenario where depth-aware data curation is most needed.\n\nTwo caveats bound this conclusion.\nFirst, the retention rates suggest that depth signals are approximately 70--85\\% text-intrinsic and 15--30\\% attributable to model self-familiarity, estimated from the off-diagonal transfer retention across continuation-mode pairs.\nThis decomposition is approximate---it assumes linearity in the accuracy-retention metric---but the magnitude is informative: the text-intrinsic component dominates, yet a nontrivial model-specific residual persists.\nThe Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo pair, which spans different architectures and training corpora, achieves the highest cross-family retention (91--92\\%), consistent with the depth signal being largely architecture-agnostic within continuation mode.\nSecond, Qwen-cont$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo retention (80--85\\%) is lower than Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo (91--92\\%), indicating that model family similarity modulates transfer even among continuation-mode configurations.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Importance Across Decoding Strategies}\n\\label{sec:discuss_features}\n\nWhile $\\Kempirical = 2$ is invariant to decoding strategy (4/4 configurations), the feature importance ordering is not uniformly consistent.\nGreedy decoding and $T{=}0.7$ share highly correlated feature rankings (Spearman $\\rho = 0.914$), with top features dominated by vocabulary diversity measures: \\texttt{self\\_bleu}, \\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}, and \\texttt{ttr}.\nIn contrast, greedy versus $T{=}1.2$ yields a lower correlation ($\\rho = 0.53$, $p = 0.041$; suggestive but nonsignificant after Bonferroni correction for 6 pairwise comparisons), with $T{=}1.2$ promoting \\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}, \\texttt{ttr}, and \\texttt{surp\\_skew} to the top ranks.\nNotably, \\texttt{freq\\_entropy} rises from rank~10 under greedy ($\\auc = 0.5139$) to rank~5 under $T{=}1.2$ ($\\auc = 0.5905$), illustrating how high-entropy decoding amplifies frequency-distribution artifacts.\n\nThis dissociation reveals an important distinction: the \\emph{existence} of the depth boundary is robust, but the \\emph{optimal feature subset} for detecting it depends on the sampling strategy.\nHigh-entropy decoding amplifies frequency-distribution artifacts while attenuating vocabulary-diversity signals, shifting which text properties carry the most discriminative power without altering whether adjacent generations can be distinguished.\nFor practitioners, this implies that a universal depth classifier should incorporate features from multiple groups---surprisal moments, vocabulary diversity, and frequency tail statistics---to maintain performance across unknown decoding conditions.\n\nThe cross-model feature ordering (Section~\\ref{sec:feature_degradation}) provides a complementary perspective: among continuation-mode models, single-feature $\\auc$ rankings are highly consistent (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.85$), with surprisal-variance features---\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}---dominating uniformly.\nA genuine limitation, however, is that depth-pair-level agreement weakens to $\\tau \\approx 0.37$ at the 1v2 boundary (Section~\\ref{sec:feature_degradation}), indicating that the relative contribution of individual features at the discrimination boundary varies across models even when the aggregate ordering is shared.\n\n\\subsection{LLM Zero-Shot Baseline}\n\\label{sec:discuss_llm}\n\nFrontier LLMs provide a natural baseline for depth estimation.\nOn a 500-sample test set with 5 depth classes (depths 0--4), GPT-5.5 achieves 33.2\\% accuracy (MAE = 1.10) and Claude Opus~4.6 achieves 33.1\\% accuracy (MAE = 1.15)---both below the OBD-LightGBM baseline of 38.6\\% (MAE = 1.11).\nSimultaneously, both models achieve approximately 88\\% binary accuracy on the human-vs.-synthetic distinction (GPT-5.5: 87.8\\%, Claude: 88.2\\%).\n\nThis gap between binary detection and depth estimation is informative.\nFrontier LLMs can reliably determine \\emph{whether} a text is machine-generated but cannot estimate \\emph{how many generations of recursive processing} it has undergone.\nDepth estimation requires sensitivity to subtle distributional shifts between adjacent generations---shifts orders of magnitude smaller than the human-vs.-machine gap.\nThe 15-dimensional statistical feature pipeline, despite its simplicity, captures these fine-grained shifts more effectively than frontier LLMs in a zero-shot setting.\nThis motivates the dedicated feature-engineering approach: the task demands specialized statistical sensitivity rather than general language understanding.\n\n\\subsection{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nWe identify six limitations that bound the generalizability of our findings.\n\n\\emph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments use models at or below 1.5B parameters (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct).\nLarger models may produce higher-fidelity text with weaker depth signatures, potentially shifting $\\Kempirical$ beyond~2.\n\n\\emph{Prompt-chain versus retrain-chain.}\nOur recursive generation protocol is a prompt-chain: the same fixed model repeatedly generates from the previous output.\nReal-world model collapse involves retrain-chains, where models are retrained on data from earlier generations~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse}.\nRetrain-chains introduce additional degradation through training dynamics, potentially producing different $\\Kstar$ values.\n\n\\emph{Language and domain.}\nAll experiments use English text from the C4 corpus.\nDepth dynamics may differ across languages, domains (e.g., code, scientific text), and source corpora with different distributional properties.\n\n\\emph{Decoding coverage.}\nWe test four decoding strategies (greedy, nucleus $p{=}0.9$, $T{=}0.7$, $T{=}1.2$).\nOther strategies---top-$k$ sampling, beam search, contrastive decoding---remain untested and may yield different feature importance patterns or boundary behavior.\n\n\\emph{Operational versus theoretical boundary.}\n$\\Kempirical$ is an operational lower bound that depends on the specific feature set and classifier.\n$\\Ktheory$ may be strictly greater; a more expressive classifier or richer feature representation could push $\\Kempirical$ higher.\n\n\\emph{Feature representation.}\nThe 15-dimensional hand-crafted feature set, while interpretable and transferable, is unlikely to be optimal.\nLearned representations---fine-tuned embeddings or probing classifiers---may capture depth-discriminative patterns that statistical features miss, potentially achieving a higher $\\Kempirical$.\n\n\\paragraph{Graduated Filtering.}\n% \\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}, and \\texttt{ttr}.\nIn contrast, greedy versus $T{=}1.2$ yields a lower correlation ($\\rho = 0.53$, $p = 0.041$; suggestive but nonsignificant after Bonferroni correction for 6 pairwise comparisons), with $T{=}1.2$ promoting \\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}, \\texttt{ttr}, and \\texttt{surp\\_skew} to the top ranks.\nNotably, \\texttt{freq\\_entropy} rises from rank~10 under greedy ($\\auc = 0.5139$) to rank~5 under $T{=}1.2$ ($\\auc = 0.5905$), illustrating how high-entropy decoding amplifies frequency-distribution artifacts.\n\nThis dissociation reveals an important distinction: the \\emph{existence} of the depth boundary is robust, but the \\emph{optimal feature subset} for detecting it depends on the sampling strategy.\nHigh-entropy decoding amplifies frequency-distribution artifacts while attenuating vocabulary-diversity signals, shifting which text properties carry the most discriminative power without altering whether adjacent generations can be distinguished.\nFor practitioners, this implies that a universal depth classifier should incorporate features from multiple groups---surprisal moments, vocabulary diversity, and frequency tail statistics---to maintain performance across unknown decoding conditions.\n\nThe cross-model feature ordering (Section~\\ref{sec:feature_degradation}) provides a complementary perspective: among continuation-mode models, single-feature $\\auc$ rankings are highly consistent (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.85$), with surprisal-variance features---\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}---dominating uniformly.\nA genuine limitation, however, is that depth-pair-level agreement weakens to $\\tau \\approx 0.37$ at the 1v2 boundary (Section~\\ref{sec:feature_degradation}), indicating that the relative contribution of individual features at the discrimination boundary varies across models even when the aggregate ordering is shared.\n\n\\subsection{LLM Zero-Shot Baseline}\n\\label{sec:discuss_llm}\n\nFrontier LLMs provide a natural baseline for depth estimation.\nOn a 500-sample test set with 5 depth classes (depths 0--4), GPT-5.5 achieves 33.2\\% accuracy (MAE = 1.10) and Claude Opus~4.6 achieves 33.1\\% accuracy (MAE = 1.15)---both below the OBD-LightGBM baseline of 38.6\\% (MAE = 1.11).\nSimultaneously, both models achieve approximately 88\\% binary accuracy on the human-vs.-synthetic distinction (GPT-5.5: 87.8\\%, Claude: 88.2\\%).\n\nThis gap between binary detection and depth estimation is informative.\nFrontier LLMs can reliably determine \\emph{whether} a text is machine-generated but cannot estimate \\emph{how many generations of recursive processing} it has undergone.\nDepth estimation requires sensitivity to subtle distributional shifts between adjacent generations---shifts orders of magnitude smaller than the human-vs.-machine gap.\nThe 15-dimensional statistical feature pipeline, despite its simplicity, captures these fine-grained shifts more effectively than frontier LLMs in a zero-shot setting.\nThis motivates the dedicated feature-engineering approach: the task demands specialized statistical sensitivity rather than general language understanding.\n\n\\subsection{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nWe identify seven limitations that bound the generalizability of our findings.\n\n\\emph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments use models at or below 1.5B parameters (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct).\nLarger models may produce higher-fidelity text with weaker depth signatures, potentially shifting $\\Kempirical$ beyond~2.\n\n\\emph{Prompt-chain versus retrain-chain.}\nOur recursive generation protocol is a prompt-chain: the same fixed model repeatedly generates from the previous output.\nReal-world model collapse involves retrain-chains, where models are retrained on data from earlier generations~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse}.\nRetrain-chains introduce additional degradation through training dynamics, potentially producing different $\\Kstar$ values.\n\n\\emph{Language and domain.}\nAll experiments use English text from the C4 corpus.\nDepth dynamics may differ across languages, domains (e.g., code, scientific text), and source corpora with different distributional properties.\n\n\\emph{Decoding coverage.}\nWe test four decoding strategies (greedy, nucleus $p{=}0.9$, $T{=}0.7$, $T{=}1.2$).\nOther strategies---top-$k$ sampling, beam search, contrastive decoding---remain untested and may yield different feature importance patterns or boundary behavior.\n\n\\emph{Operational versus theoretical boundary.}\n$\\Kempirical$ is an operational lower bound that depends on the specific feature set and classifier.\n$\\Ktheory$ may be strictly greater; a more expressive classifier or richer feature representation could push $\\Kempirical$ higher.\n\n\\emph{Feature representation.}\nThe 15-dimensional hand-crafted feature set, while interpretable and transferable, is unlikely to be optimal.\nLearned representations---fine-tuned embeddings or probing classifiers---may capture depth-discriminative patterns that statistical features miss, potentially achieving a higher $\\Kempirical$.\n\n\\paragraph{Graduated Filtering.}\n% [TODO: exp-006 graduated filtering results pending.\n% Will discuss: 6 strategies × 5 seeds × α sweep results,\n% graduated vs binary filtering comparison on MMLU/HellaSwag,\n% practical implications of coarse 3-class ordinal signal for data curation.]\n",

  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Graduated Filtering.}\n% TODO: exp-006 graduated filtering 结果填充\n\n\\subsection{Broader Impact}\n\\label{sec:broader_impact}\n\nThe $\\Kempirical = 2$ boundary has implications beyond the immediate scope of depth classification.\n\n\\paragraph{Synthetic data curation.}\nThe practical consequence of $\\Kempirical = 2$ is a three-tier taxonomy of text quality: human-authored (depth~0), first-generation synthetic (depth~1), and deeper synthetic (depth~$\\geq 2$).\nTexts at depth~$\\geq 2$ are mutually indistinguishable and can be treated as a single category for curation purposes.\nFor practitioners filtering training data, this implies that a single round of synthetic rewriting suffices to make text statistically indistinguishable from text that has undergone multiple rounds of recycling---a form of one-wash laundering that limits the granularity of quality-based filtering.\nGraduated filtering strategies that exploit this trichotomy may outperform binary human/synthetic filters, as the coarse ordinal signal (human / shallow / deep) captures the dominant axis of quality variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Relation to model collapse.}\nShumailov et al.~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse} and Alemohammad et al.~\\cite{alemohammad2024selfconsuming} study model collapse under \\emph{retrain-chains}, where models are iteratively trained on their own outputs.\nOur prompt-chain protocol measures an adjacent but distinct phenomenon: the degradation of statistical distinguishability under recursive generation by a fixed model.\nThe rapid convergence we observe ($\\Kempirical = 2$) is consistent with the theoretical prediction of convergence to a fixed point, but the precise relationship between prompt-chain $\\Kempirical$ and retrain-chain convergence rates remains an open question.\nIf prompt-chain depth signals serve as an observable proxy for early-stage model collapse, depth estimation could provide a lightweight diagnostic for data curation pipelines without requiring expensive retraining experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{AI text detection.}\nCurrent AI text detection is predominantly binary: human versus machine.\nOur results suggest that \\emph{depth-aware} detection provides a strictly more informative signal.\nThe near-perfect 0v1 separability ($\\auc > 0.99$) confirms that binary detection is reliable at this scale, but the 1v2 boundary ($\\auc$: 0.61--0.70) reveals that first-generation text retains measurable statistical distance from deeper synthetic text.\nThis additional dimension---how many generations of processing a text has undergone---may prove valuable for provenance tracking and corpus quality assessment, where the binary human/AI label elides important distinctions between lightly and heavily recycled content."
}

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Theoretical Implications of $\\Kempirical = 2$}\n\\label{sec:discuss_kstar}\n\nThe Data Processing Inequality guarantees the existence of a finite $\\Ktheory$ beyond which adjacent generations become indistinguishable (Section~\\ref{sec:formulation}).\nOur empirical result---$\\Kempirical = 2$ uniformly across all 8 tested configurations (4 model-mode combinations from Section~\\ref{sec:kstar_boundary} and 4 decoding strategies from the ablation)---establishes that this boundary is not only finite but remarkably shallow at the $\\leq$1.5B parameter scale.\n\nThe shallowness of $\\Kempirical$ is explained by the rate of information loss across generations.\nThe 0v1 boundary is near-trivially separable ($\\auc > 0.99$ across all configurations), reflecting the large distributional gap between human text and first-generation synthetic text.\nAt the 1v2 boundary, $\\auc$ drops by approximately 30 percentage points ($\\auc$ range: 0.6112--0.6955), indicating that the majority of the discriminative signal is consumed in the first generation step.\nBy the 2v3 boundary, $\\auc$ falls to 0.5496--0.5893---below the operational threshold $\\theta = 0.60$---and deeper pairs converge toward chance.\nRecursive generation thus acts as a severely lossy channel whose information features---surprisal variance (\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std}) and its derivatives---all capture the heterogeneity of token-level predictability.\nHuman text exhibits high surprisal variance reflecting diverse syntactic structures and unpredictable discourse patterns.\nThe first generation step smooths this variance substantially, replacing high-surprisal tokens and atypical constructions with more predictable alternatives; the second step reduces the remaining variance to near-floor levels, leaving insufficient statistical fingerprint for reliable adjacent-depth discrimination.\nCrucially, this degradation ordering is consistent across model families (Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.85$ for all continuation-mode pairs), suggesting that the two-step information loss is governed by the autoregressive generation mechanism itself rather than by model-specific biases.\n\nThis pattern aligns with findings from the model collapse literature.\nShumailov et al.~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse} observed that tail-distribution degradation is most acute in the earliest generations, with minority modes vanishing before the output distribution stabilizes.\nDohmatob et al.~\\cite{dohmatob2024model} derived scaling laws demonstrating that tail probability mass is lost at rates predictable from model and data scale.\nOur $\\Kempirical = 2$ provides a complementary, classifier-based measurement of this phenomenon: the information-theoretic discrimination horizon is reached after just two generation steps.\nAlemohammad et al.~\\cite{alemohammad2024selfconsuming} proved convergence to a fixed point for self-consuming generative models; $\\Kempirical = 2$ indicates that, operationally, the approach to this fixed point is generation process rather than by surface-level properties of the sampling algorithm.\n\n\\subsection{Implications of Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:discuss_transfer}\n\nThe cross-model transfer results (Section~\\ref{sec:transfer}) indicate that depth signals are largely encoded in text-level statistical properties rather than generator-specific artifacts.\nClassifiers trained on Pythia-1.4B and evaluated on OLMo-1B retain 90--92\\% of within-model accuracy, and the broader continuation-mode transfer matrix shows 80--92\\% retention across all model pairs.\nAt the 0v1 boundary, cross-model $\\auc$ exceeds 0.96 universally, confirming that binary contamination detection is fully transferable across generators.\n\nThe practical implication is direct: a single depth classifier trained on one model's outputs can be deployed to estimate generational depth for texts produced by other models, without model-specific retraining.\nThis transferability extends the utility of depth estimation to open-web settings where the generating model is unknown---precisely the scenario where depth-aware data curation is most needed.\n\nTwo caveats bound this conclusion.\nFirst, the
